A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n\n**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\nThe danger was never the guess, it is that a guess and a measurement look identical six\nmonths later. `how` is still required, because \"eyeballed against a broom\" tells the\nnext person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\nsomebody picks up a caliper.\n\nA measured value pays for itself across a family. Notch measured one bracket pitch and\namortized it over sixteen parts.\n\n## Assemblies\n\nEvery rule above judges one solid being manufactured. An assembly judges solids being\nused together: a door on its mount, a lid over the machine it covers. The failure it\nexists for built clean, checked clean, exported watertight and printed beautifully,\nthen jammed at 45 degrees of its swing, because the collision between two correct\nparts existed only in the physical world. No per-solid rule can have an opinion about\nmotion.\n\nThe contract mirrors a part's. An assembly is a function in `parts/` whose keyword\ndefaults are its parameters; what it returns is placed solids rather than one solid.\n`use(name)` builds a sibling part where it was modelled, `hinge(solid, axis, through,\nat)` declares a revolute joint and poses it, and `obstacle(solid, name)` adds what the\nparts mount into: the machine, the wall, the shelf above. The joint angle is an\nordinary keyword default, so the viewer's slider swings it live, and the slider ends\nexactly where the declared range does.\n\n**The declared range is a claim, and `nurb check` audits it** the way min_wall audits\na wall: each hinge sweeps through it against everything else in the scene, and a\nfinding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. Declare what\nthe design needs, never what happens to pass. A door that must stay open by gravity\nneeds past 90 degrees; declaring (0, 90) because that sweeps clean is writing the test\nto match the bug.\n\n**Nothing full width may cross the pivot line.** The mount that carries a hinge has to\nreach the pin from somewhere, so some of it always lives above or behind the pin, and\nany full-width material on the moving part that reaches past the pivot sweeps straight\ninto it. The door that jammed at 45 did so against its own top edge, carried past the\npin to support the knuckles; the knuckles got narrow tabs instead, sized to pass\nbeside the mount, and the panel now starts at the pivot line.\n\n**Obstacles are boxes and prisms, never thin hand-drawn profiles.** Three nearly\ncollinear points make a razor sliver, and OCCT answers a boolean against a degenerate\nsolid with chunks of the other operand: measured once as a 152,000mm3 phantom\ncollision at a pose that was actually clear. The sweep refuses any intersection that\nis not a subset of its inputs and names the solid to fix, but the fix is to model the\nobstacle as something with honest volume in the first place.\n\n**Tangent is clear, and clear is not clearance.** Collision is intersection volume, so\ntwo faces that kiss at zero volume pass, the closed door resting on its stop included.\nIn plastic a zero-clearance pass is already a bind. The declared range should carry\nthe same honesty about clearance that a tongue's `fit` carries about width. The fits\ntable puts numbers to that honesty: 0.3mm is the least a moving pair carries through\nits whole travel, and 0.5mm is what never binds.\n\n**The stop the sweep finds can be the detent the design wants.** The door that\nmotivated all of this rests open at 240 degrees against the back of its own mount,\nheld there by gravity, and that contact is a feature with a card entry, not a finding\nto engineer away. Read a jam before redesigning around it.\n\n**Never trust a swing worked out by hand.** The same hinge was hand-modelled three times and gave three answers, one of them \"impossible\"; the wrong one ran a convex test against a non-convex profile, which is exactly the kind of quiet modelling error a kernel boolean cannot make. The sweep is the authority.\n\n**The sweep is priced per pose, and the declaration sets the pose count.** Every step is a kernel boolean against the rest of the scene, so a door walking 90 degrees at the default 3-degree step is cheap, while a full-circle rotor at the same step is 120 poses and can hold the check for seconds on real geometry. A clean sweep pays the whole walk, since only a jam stops it early. `through=(0, 360)` at the default step is a fine-grained jam audit, not a formality: a free spinner that only needs a gross clearance check should declare a larger `step`, and a range wider than the design actually claims buys nothing but poses.\n\nOne hinge sweeps at a time; the others hold their pose, which is the conservative\nreading of a mechanism you can only move one hand at a time. Printability rules stay\noff assemblies entirely: each part already answered them alone, and overhang measured\non an assembled scene reports confident nonsense.\n\n## Verification\n\n\"It built\" is not verification. `nurb verify` runs the machine-checkable part of\nthis list: one solid per configuration, every count flexed upward, the rules clean,\nand the card agreeing with the geometry. `--report` writes the verdict into\n`build/renders/<part>.verify.md` with the renders that back it beside it, one still\nper finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n\nBefore presenting a part:\n\n1. **Flex the driving parameters up as well as down**, for example 4 to 6 to 2 to 4.\n   Growth is what catches a frozen selector; shrinking alone passes a broken part.\n2. **Check fit-critical faces by coordinate** after any polish or resize. `nurb inspect`\n   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. Which faces are\n   fit-critical is still yours to know, which is why this is not something `nurb verify`\n   can run for you.\n3. **Confirm the solid count**, which is almost always one.\n4. **Run `nurb check`.** Zero findings is the bar, and a finding fired at a part that\n   prints fine is a bug in the rule, not something to accept quietly.\n5. **Predict a baseline before you look at it.** Working out what the sliver count\n   should be from the polish exclusions turns the number into a test of the rule instead\n   of something to write down.\n6. **Render it and actually look.** `nurb render <part>` writes a PNG, `--section\n   z:4mm` cuts it open where outside views cannot reach, and `nurb inspect --render`\n   stands a camera at every finding with the guilty face painted. A part can pass\n   every numeric check and still be visibly wrong.","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01FJ5meh2EfRmdwwHeacpA5r","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Support-free counterbores: the stepped bridging hole.\n\nA counterbored hole prints mouth toward the bed, because that is where the screw head\ncomes from. That floats its ceiling: the shelf the head bears on is laid flat over the\nopen bore, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air. Slicers print it\nbadly or demand support inside a pocket nobody can clean.\n\nThe print-farm fix is sequential bridging, two sacrificial layers between bore and\nhole. First a slot the hole's width, bridged chord-to-chord across the bore; then the\nsame slot turned ninety degrees, bridged across the first; then the round hole starts\nover the crossing, ringed by material that bridges the short way at every point. Each\nlayer spans only what the one before it laid, so the whole stack prints support-free\nand the screw never notices: the steps sit above the head's seat and every opening\nclears the shaft.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom build123d import Align, Box, Cylinder, Pos\n\n# How far the cutter reaches past its own mouth, so a caller seating the mouth flush\n# on the part's bottom face never leaves a coplanar-face boolean to the kernel's mood.\nLEAD = 1.0\n\n_SEATED = (Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN)\n\n\ndef counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth, layer=1.0):\n    \"\"\"The negative space of a counterbored hole whose ceiling prints without support.\n\n    Returns a cutter to subtract: mouth centred at the origin opening downward, hole\n    rising up +z, which is a counterbore printed the way one is used, head toward the\n    bed. `hole_dia` is the shaft's hole, `head_dia` and `head_depth` the head's pocket,\n    `depth` the full reach of the hole from the mouth. Position it at the part's bottom\n    face; the mouth runs 1mm long so a flush seat still cuts clean, and `depth` can\n    be generous for a through hole, the excess sticks out the top harmlessly.\n\n    Between pocket and hole sit two bridge steps, each `layer` tall: a slot across the\n    bore, then the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first. Anything at or\n    above the printed layer height bridges; the default is 1mm, exactly the printer's\n    min_wall, so the sacrificial laminae between the steps never read as thin walls to\n    the checker. Shrink it only when depth is tight, and expect min_wall to notice.\n    \"\"\"\n    if hole_dia <= 0 or head_dia <= 0 or head_depth <= 0 or layer <= 0:\n        raise ValueError(\"counterbore() needs positive dimensions throughout\")\n    if head_dia <= hole_dia:\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"a counterbore needs a head pocket wider than its hole, got head_dia \"\n            f\"{head_dia:g} against hole_dia {hole_dia:g}. For a plain hole, cut a Cylinder.\"\n        )\n    if depth < head_depth + 2 * layer + 1e-9:\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"depth {depth:g} leaves no hole above the pocket: the head takes \"\n            f\"{head_depth:g} and the two bridge steps take {2 * layer:g}\"\n        )\n    pocket = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, head_depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n    # The steps stay inside the pocket's circle: a slot cut square would notch the wall.\n    inside = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, 2 * layer, align=_SEATED)\n    first = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Box(head_dia, hole_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    second = Pos(0, 0, head_depth + layer) * Box(hole_dia, head_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    shaft = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(hole_dia / 2, depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n    return pocket + first + second + shaft","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"f905d554-2e45-42aa-bd51-0c81461d5e6b","uuid":"b39ea3e4-2b79-4693-89aa-15e3d8b8748c","timestamp":"2026-08-25T22:30:04.765Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"\"\"\"Support-free counterbores: the stepped bridging hole.\n\nA counterbored hole prints mouth toward the bed, because that is where the screw head\ncomes from. That floats its ceiling: the shelf the head bears on is laid flat over the\nopen bore, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air. Slicers print it\nbadly or demand support inside a pocket nobody can clean.\n\nThe print-farm fix is sequential bridging, two sacrificial layers between bore and\nhole. First a slot the hole's width, bridged chord-to-chord across the bore; then the\nsame slot turned ninety degrees, bridged across the first; then the round hole starts\nover the crossing, ringed by material that bridges the short way at every point. Each\nlayer spans only what the one before it laid, so the whole stack prints support-free\nand the screw never notices: the steps sit above the head's seat and every opening\nclears the shaft.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom build123d import Align, Box, Cylinder, Pos\n\n# How far the cutter reaches past its own mouth, so a caller seating the mouth flush\n# on the part's bottom face never leaves a coplanar-face boolean to the kernel's mood.\nLEAD = 1.0\n\n_SEATED = (Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN)\n\n\ndef counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth, layer=1.0):\n    \"\"\"The negative space of a counterbored hole whose ceiling prints without support.\n\n    Returns a cutter to subtract: mouth centred at the origin opening downward, hole\n    rising up +z, which is a counterbore printed the way one is used, head toward the\n    bed. `hole_dia` is the shaft's hole, `head_dia` and `head_depth` the head's pocket,\n    `depth` the full reach of the hole from the mouth. Position it at the part's bottom\n    face; the mouth runs 1mm long so a flush seat still cuts clean, and `depth` can\n    be generous for a through hole, the excess sticks out the top harmlessly.\n\n    Between pocket and hole sit two bridge steps, each `layer` tall: a slot across the\n    bore, then the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first. Anything at or\n    above the printed layer height bridges; the default is 1mm, exactly the printer's\n    min_wall, so the sacrificial laminae between the steps never read as thin walls to\n    the checker. Shrink it only when depth is tight, and expect min_wall to notice.\n    \"\"\"\n    if hole_dia <= 0 or head_dia <= 0 or head_depth <= 0 or layer <= 0:\n        raise ValueError(\"counterbore() needs positive dimensions throughout\")\n    if head_dia <= hole_dia:\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"a counterbore needs a head pocket wider than its hole, got head_dia \"\n            f\"{head_dia:g} against hole_dia {hole_dia:g}. For a plain hole, cut a Cylinder.\"\n        )\n    if depth < head_depth + 2 * layer + 1e-9:\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"depth {depth:g} leaves no hole above the pocket: the head takes \"\n            f\"{head_depth:g} and the two bridge steps take {2 * layer:g}\"\n        )\n    pocket = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, head_depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n    # The steps stay inside the pocket's circle: a slot cut square would notch the wall.\n    inside = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, 2 * layer, align=_SEATED)\n    first = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Box(head_dia, hole_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    second = Pos(0, 0, head_depth + layer) * Box(hole_dia, head_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    shaft = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(hole_dia / 2, depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n    return pocket + first + second + shaft","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

An edge belongs to the shape it was selected from\n    and `chamfer` reads its target off that shape, so applying batches in sequence would\n    quietly keep re-chamfering the first body and hand it back.\n\n    This does not choose what to polish. Deciding which edges are exposed, which are\n    mating geometry and which are concave is the part's judgement and stays in the part.\n    \"\"\"\n    floor = 0.8 * size**2\n\n    def tiny(solid):\n        return len([f for f in solid.faces() if f.area < floor])\n\n    allowance = tiny(shape)\n    kept, best = [], shape\n\n    def lands(candidate):\n        try:\n            # The raw kernel call, not the guarded one: bisecting expects failures and\n            # discards them, so building the guidance text here would be advice nobody\n            # reads, once per attempt.\n            out = _chamfer(candidate, size)\n        except Exception:\n            return None\n        return out if tiny(out) <= allowance else None\n\n    def take(batch):\n        nonlocal kept, best\n--- measurements.py ---\n\"\"\"Named real-world values, with provenance.\n\nThe bottleneck on a one-off part is not geometry, it is dimensions. An agent cannot\nmeasure a freezer shelf, and a guessed number produces a perfect model of the wrong\nobject: it builds, it checks clean, and it prints. Nothing downstream catches it.\n\nSo a measurement is a named value on disk with a note saying how it was obtained, and\nasking for one that is not on file raises. That failure is the feature. It is the moment\nto ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport pathlib\nimport sys\nimport tomllib\n\nFILENAME = \"measurements.toml\"\n\n\nclass MeasurementError(LookupError):\n    \"\"\"A measurement is missing or unusable.\n\n    Not `KeyError`, which reprs its argument: a message with a worked example in it comes\n    out as one quoted string full of backslash-n, and the example is the useful part.\n    \"\"\"\n\n\ndef _caller_dir(depth):\n    \"\"\"Where the code that called us lives, so the lookup needs no configuration.\"\"\"\n    frame = sys._getframe(depth)\n    here = frame.f_globals.get(\"__file__\")\n    return pathlib.Path(here).resolve().parent if here else pathlib.Path.cwd()\n\n\ndef _find(start):\n    \"\"\"The nearest measurements file, without leaving the project.\n\n    The walk stops at the project root, meaning the directory holding `parts/`. Without\n    that stop it would keep going to the filesystem root and could silently answer with\n    some unrelated file from an ancestor directory, which is the exact failure this whole\n    module exists to prevent: a dimension that is wrong and looks fine.\n    \"\"\"\n    for d in [start, *start.parents]:\n        if (d / FILENAME).is_file():\n            return d / FILENAME\n        if (d / \"parts\").is_dir():\n            return None\n    return None\n\n\ndef _read(path):\n    \"\"\"Parse it, and say which file when it will not parse. Empty when there is no file.\"\"\"\n    path = _find(pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2))\n    return _read(path) if path else {}\n\n\ndef provisional(start=None):\n    \"\"\"Every value on file that admits it was not actually measured.\n\n    The doctrine says to ask rather than improvise, and sometimes there is nobody to\n    ask: the caliper is in a drawer, the object is at the office, the print has to go\n    on tonight. The failure mode is not putting a number in, it is that the number then\n    looks exactly like one somebody measured. Six months later nothing distinguishes\n    them and the part is wrong for a reason no one can find.\n\n    So a guess is allowed to be written down and required to say so, and `nurb check`\n    reports the ones that still are. `how` stays mandatory either way: a provisional\n    value still has to say where it came from, because \"eyeballed against a broom\"\n    tells the next person how much to trust it.\n    \"\"\"\n    return sorted(\n        (name, entry.get(\"how\", \"\"))\n        for name, entry in load(start).items()\n        if entry.get(\"provisional\")\n    )\n\n\ndef measured(name, start=None):\n    \"\"\"A measured value, in mm.\n\n    Raises when it is not on file, when it has no provenance, or when it is recorded in\n    units this does not work in. All three are cases where continuing would produce\n    geometry that looks right and is wrong.\n\n    A value marked `provisional = true` is returned like any other. It is a real number\n    that builds a real part; what it is not is measured, and `nurb check` says so until\n    somebody picks up a caliper.\n    \"\"\"\n    start = pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2)\n    path = _find(start)\n    if path is None:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"no {FILENAME} above {start}, so there is nothing measured to ask for. \"\n            f\"Measure {name!r}, then record it:\\n\\n\"\n            f'  [{name}]\\n  value = 0\\n  unit = \"mm\"\\n  how = \"how you measured it\"\\n\\n'\n            f\"If there is nobody to ask right now, add `provisional = true` and put \"\n            f\"your best guess in. `nurb check` will keep reminding you.\"\n        )\n    book = _read(path)\n    if name not in book:\n        have = \", \".join(sorted(book)) or \"nothing yet\"\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"nothing measured called {name!r} in {path.name}. have: {have}. \"\n            f\"Ask for the measurement rather than guessing one: a wrong number here \"\n            f\"builds, checks clean, and prints.\"\n        )\n    entry = book[name]\n    if \"value\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} has no value\")\n    if \"how\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"{path.name}: {name} does not say how it was measured. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-5","id":"msg_011CeQGC3BkxDpDW9ku2PLhx","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_019crmTPrgVR5DUXYmgjFwkC","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-asqti2ig/project/parts/leg_cup.md","content":"# leg_cup\n\n## What it is\n\nA slip-over foot cup for the workbench's short leg. It sits on the floor; the leg's\nsquare foot drops into the top-opening pocket, and the solid floor under the pocket\nlifts that corner of the bench level.\n\n## Design notes\n\nPocket is `leg_width`/`leg_depth` (measured off the leg) plus 0.4mm clearance on each\naxis, 8mm deep, walls 2mm thick on all four sides. All three driving numbers —\n`leg_width`, `leg_depth`, `lift` — come from `measurements.toml` via `measured()`, not\nfrom part parameters, so editing the file alone is enough to retarget the part.\n\n`lift`, the floor thickness, is `provisional`: the bench is at the shop overnight and\nthe actual wobble was never measured, only bracketed between 2.0 and 5.0mm by the shim\nthat used to be wedged under the leg. 3.5mm (the midpoint) is the working guess until\nsomeone puts a feeler gauge under the leg and records a real number.\n\nOnly the four outer vertical corners are chamfered. The top and bottom perimeters are\nleft sharp on purpose: chamfering all three convex edges that meet at a top corner\nmints the classic 0.87mm2 corner-triangle sliver, and chamfering the pocket's top\nopening would be a lead-in chamfer on mating geometry, which the doctrine forbids\noutright. Leaving both alone keeps the part clean on `nurb check` and keeps the pocket\nmouth untouched for the leg to seat in.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Don't chamfer the top or bottom face perimeters. Doing so either mints a\n  corner-triangle sliver (top, where a vertical and two top edges would all be\n  chamfered at once) or touches the bottom bed face. Vertical corners alone give the\n  part its faceted look without either problem.\n- Don't add a lead-in chamfer to the pocket mouth. It looks helpful and it is exactly\n  the fit-critical mating geometry the polish pass has to leave alone.\n- Don't bake `lift` (or `leg_width`/`leg_depth`) into the part as a literal or a\n  parameter default. It sits on the floor; the leg's\nsquare foot drops into the top-opening pocket, and the solid floor under the pocket\nlifts that corner of the bench level.\n\n## Design notes\n\nPocket is `leg_width`/`leg_depth` (measured off the leg) plus 0.4mm clearance on each\naxis, 8mm deep, walls 2mm thick on all four sides. All three driving numbers —\n`leg_width`, `leg_depth`, `lift` — come from `measurements.toml` via `measured()`, not\nfrom part parameters, so editing the file alone is enough to retarget the part.\n\n`lift`, the floor thickness, is `provisional`: the bench is at the shop overnight and\nthe actual wobble was never measured, only bracketed between 2.0 and 5.0mm by the shim\nthat used to be wedged under the leg. 3.5mm (the midpoint) is the working guess until\nsomeone puts a feeler gauge under the leg and records a real number.\n\nOnly the four outer vertical corners are chamfered. The top and bottom perimeters are\nleft sharp on purpose: chamfering all three convex edges that meet at a top corner\nmints the classic 0.87mm2 corner-triangle sliver, and chamfering the pocket's top\nopening would be a lead-in chamfer on mating geometry, which the doctrine forbids\noutright. Leaving both alone keeps the part clean on `nurb check` and keeps the pocket\nmouth untouched for the leg to seat in.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Don't chamfer the top or bottom face perimeters. Doing so either mints a\n  corner-triangle sliver (top, where a vertical and two top edges would all be\n  chamfered at once) or touches the bottom bed face. Vertical corners alone give the\n  part its faceted look without either problem.\n- Don't add a lead-in chamfer to the pocket mouth.
